- Champion: -1.15 (big gap to 2nd)

- 6th onwards: -0.07 to 3.15

The champion uses: GoToColor(cyan=6) → detect red(1) → GoAwayColor(gray=1) → GoToColor(gray=1). The key insight seems to be going toward cyan (the source area wall colors?), avoiding red (nest wall), and navigating via gray floor.

Wait, let me re-read color codes:
- clr colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- LED colors: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

The champion (state 0): GoToColor cyan (clr=6), transitions when sees red (l=1, color 1=red). This makes sense - go toward cyan walls (source area), when red wall (nest side) is seen, transition.

State 1: GoAwayColor gray (clr=1=red? No wait - clr1=1 means color 1 which is red). GoAwayColor red (run away from red wall = move toward source). Transitions on gray floor (C=1) or few neighbors (C=4).

State 2: GoToColor gray (clr=1=red again). With transitions on seeing yellow (l=4) which might be robot LEDs.

The champion seems to be navigating by: go toward cyan walls (source), avoid red wall (nest), pick up items. But it's scoring -1.15 meaning it's delivering beneficial items more than harmful ones.

**Strategy for Round 2:**

1. **Survival**: Copy champion exactly
2. **Exploit champion**: Vary probabilities and parameters slightly
3. **Explore**: Try explicit foraging patterns - go to black floor (source), return to white floor (nest), avoid green side

Key insight: One half of source DECREASES score (good), other INCREASES (bad). We want robots to go to the GOOD half. The good half is either blue or green walled. We need to figure out which - but we can't know without testing. Let's try machines that favor one color.

For exploitation, I'll vary the champion's parameters. For exploration, I'll try:
- Machines that go to black (source) and then white (nest) with floor-color transitions
- Machines targeting specific wall colors (blue=3 or green=2) to find the good side

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion (-4.68) uses:
- State 0: GoToColor **blue** (clr=3), transition on BLACK floor (p=0.85) or seeing RED (l=1, p=0.6)
- State 1: GoToColor **red** (clr=1=red? No - wait, clr=1 means color 1), transition on WHITE floor (p=0.75) or seeing RED (p=0.5) → back to state 0
- State 2: GoToColor **red** (clr=1), transition on WHITE floor (p=0.7) or BLACK floor (p=0.4) → back to state 0/1

Wait, let me re-read: colors for GoToColor: {1,2,3,4,5,6}. The arena has RED wall (nest side), BLUE walls (one source half), GREEN walls (other source half). 

Champion: GoToColor blue (clr=3) → on black floor go to state 1; or see red go to state 1.
State 1: GoToColor red (clr=1) → on white floor go to state 1 (self? No, --n1x0 1 = destination index 1 among others [0,2], so destination is state 2); see red → state 0.
State 2: GoToColor red (clr=1) → white floor → state 1; black floor → state 0.

This makes sense: **Go to blue side** (score-decreasing item), pick up item on black floor, then **go toward red wall** (navigate to nest/white floor), deliver, repeat.

The very bad score 4.07 (13th) used clr=2 (green) instead of clr=3 (blue). This confirms: **BLUE side is good, GREEN side is bad**. The champion correctly targets BLUE walls.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion topology with parameter variations
3. Try adding more transitions to champion-like machines
4. Explore 4-state variants with clearer go-to-blue → deliver cycle

The champion structure is essentially:
- GoToBlue → when on black (source), transition to GoToRed
- GoToRed → when on white (nest), transition back
- Extra GoToRed state for robustness

Let me exploit this heavily with parameter tweaks and some topology variations.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

Big score jump: champion is -11.37, 2nd is -11.07, 3rd is -10.46. Then a large gap to 4th (-5.66). Then another huge gap from 8th (-5.44) to 9th (0.17).

The top 3 machines share a clear structure:
- State 0: GoToColor **blue** (clr=3), transition on BLACK floor or seeing RED
- State 1: GoToColor **red** (clr=1), transition on WHITE floor or seeing RED/blue
- States 2+: GoToColor red or GoAwayColor green (clr=2)

The champion (4-state) is:
- S0: GoToBlue → black floor→S1, see red→S2
- S1: GoToRed → white floor→S2, see red→S0, see blue→S2
- S2: GoToRed → white floor→S0, black floor→S1
- S3: GoAwayGreen → black floor→S0, white floor→S2

State 3 (GoAwayGreen) makes sense! It actively avoids the green (bad) side.

The 2nd best (-11.07, 3-state) has:
- S0: GoToBlue → black floor (p=0.92), white floor (p=0.03 - rare)
- S1: GoToRed → white floor, see red→S0, see blue→S0
- S2: GoToRed → white floor, black floor→S0

The 3rd best (-10.46) has GoAwayGreen as state 2 instead of GoToRed.

**Key pattern**: GoToBlue + GoToRed (nest navigation) + something extra. The champion's extra state (GoAwayGreen) seems to help actively avoid the bad side.

**Exploitation strategy**: Vary probabilities in top 3 machines, add transitions, try GoAwayGreen more.

**Important correction I notice**: In the champion, `--n1x2 2` means transition K=2 from state 1 goes to destination index 2. With 4 states and leaving state 1, the others are [0,2,3] → D=0→S0, D=1→S2, D=2→S3. So `--n1x2 2` → S3 (GoAwayGreen). That's interesting.

Let me design exploitations carefully:

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The champion (-16.27) has a distinctive feature: state 0 transitions to state 2 on BLACK floor with very low probability (p=0.05) rather than on seeing RED. This is different from all other machines. Let me analyze:

- S0: GoToBlue (clr=3) → black floor→S1 (p=0.85), black floor→S2 (p=0.05) [two transitions, same condition C=0!]

Wait - actually C=0 is "floor is BLACK" for transition 0, and C=0 is "floor is BLACK" for transition 1. That's two BLACK floor transitions! This means when on black floor, it probabilistically chooses S1 (85%) or S2 (5%). The 5% case sends it to S2 (GoToRed) directly without going through S1.

- S1: GoToRed → white floor→S2, see red→S0, see blue→S2 (destination index 2 from state 1 with 4 states = S3)
- S2: GoToRed → white floor→S0, black floor→S1
- S3: GoAwayGreen → black floor→S0, white floor→S2

The big gap is between rank 1 (-16.27) and rank 2 (-11.73). The key difference in the champion is `--c0x1 0 --p0x1 0.05` (transition to S2 on black floor, rare) instead of `--c0x1 7 --p0x1 0.6 --l0x1 1` (transition on seeing red).

This means S0 sometimes goes directly to S2 (GoToRed) when it hits black floor. This could help robots that stumble onto the wrong side quickly redirect.

**Strategy**: Focus heavily on exploiting the champion structure. The key insight is the double black-floor transition in S0. Let me try variations:
1. Champion exact copy
2. Vary probabilities around the champion
3. Try the champion structure with additional transitions
4. Combine champion with the 3-state structure from rank 4 (also -11.39)
5. Try slightly different probability combos for the champion structure

The ranks 2-11 are all between -9.56 and -11.73, relatively close. The champion stands out. Let me focus on variations of the champion's unique double-black-floor transition in S0.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 6:**

Scores are very tightly clustered (-12.35 to -17.27), with a large gap from rank 11 (-12.35) to rank 12 (-5.38). The structure is now very well established:
- S0: GoToBlue (clr=3), black→S1 (p≈0.85), black→S2 (p≈0.05-0.06)
- S1: GoToRed (clr=1), white→S2, see red→S0, see blue(3)→S3
- S2: GoToRed (clr=1), white→S0, black→S1, see green(2)→S3
- S3: GoAwayGreen (clr=2), black→S0, white→S2

The champion has p0x1=0.06 (slightly higher than 0.05 in most others). The p2x2 (see green→S3) = 0.45 in champion vs 0.5 in others. These small differences matter.

**What hurts**: 
- Adding extra transition in S3 seeing blue→S0 (rank 12, -5.38) - bad!
- Adding extra transition in S3 seeing blue→S1 (rank 13, -1.75) - very bad!
- Adding 4th transition in S2 (rank 10, -12.68) - slightly worse
- Adding 4th transition in S1 seeing green (rank 11, -12.35) - worse

**What helps**: 
- p0x1=0.06 (champion) > 0.05 - worth trying 0.07, 0.08
- p2x2=0.45 (champion) - try 0.35-0.50
- p3x1 (white→S2 in GoAwayGreen) = 0.6 - try variations

The topology is essentially fixed. Focus on fine-tuning probabilities around the champion:
- p0x0: 0.82-0.90
- p0x1: 0.04-0.08
- p1x0: 0.72-0.82
- p1x1: 0.45-0.55
- p1x2: 0.45-0.55
- p2x0: 0.65-0.75
- p2x1: 0.35-0.45
- p2x2: 0.35-0.55
- p3x0: 0.75-0.88
- p3x1: 0.55-0.65

Also try some exploratory changes: different GoAwayGreen transitions in S3, or adding a white-floor trigger in S0.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
